8 Easy Landscape Designer Tricks to Transition Your Garden From Fall into Winter

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

What Your Jade Plant Needs in October – 7 Tips to Keep It Gorgeous and Glossy Through Winter

These 7 Fall Cleanup Mistakes Could Ruin Next Year's Garden – Here's What to Do Instead

5 Easy Fall Wins to Help Your Backyard Birds Survive Winter – How to Beat the Big Chill and Ensure More Visits

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

A few thoughtful changes will keep your garden landscape looking effortlessly stylish through the cooler seasons.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While you might be thinking that the gardening season is drawing to a close this month, there's plenty to look forward to, especially if make a few simple switches to help your yard transition from fall into winter. As the year segues into the cooler months, it's time to enjoy colorful stems, textural bark, sculptural seedheads and shrubs bejewelled with bright berries, and relish the quiet beauty of this season.

Check through our landscape designer tricks to take a fall yard into winter, and add one or two to your outside space now. An injection of structural evergreens, naturalistic ornamental grasses, a tree with fiery foliage or colorful autumn bulbs can make a huge difference at this time of year now the late-season flower show is coming to an end.

Consider, too, how to make your fall garden user-friendly so you feel inspired to spend plenty of time outside still, perhaps by including a cosy gathering area around a fire pit. Which of these easy tricks landscape designers use to carry a garden from fall into winter are right for your yard?

Adding a mix of ornamental grasses is a fundamental element of fall garden design and these low-maintenance plants will continue to look good all through winter. While many annual and perennial flowering plants will happily grow through airy grasses in summer, the blades will keep borders looking full once they've finish blooming.

Choose varieties that offer bold autumn color such as Panicum virgatum ‘Shenandoah’, available from Nature Hills. Also known as switch grass, this well-behaved clump-forming grass develops stunning red and damson tones in fall, topped by wiry stems of flowers. Reaching 3 feet high, it looks fabulous planted among taller shrubs as well as with perennial blooms.

Another option favored by landscape designers is blue-green-leaved Festuca glauca 'Elijah Blue', also available from Nature Hills. Growing to just a foot high, it's perfect for border edges and winter containers.

Landscape designers often choose trees with fiery foliage to create a stunning focal point with brilliant shades of scarlet, russet, gold or burgundy. Even if space is limited, there are plenty of smaller trees that will squeeze into a compact garden.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Some of most spectacular fall color is typically associated with Acer (maple) trees, but to carry the interest on into winter, choose a type with textural bark, too. Acer griseum, the paperbark maple and available from Plant Addicts, has peeling, papery warm-brown bark. Caught in the light of a low winter sun, its peeling curls of bark are nothing short of spectacular. The coral bark Japanese maple, available from trees.com, has bark in an intense red tone that's equally eye-catching.

Another option if you're wondering what to plant in fall is a multi-trunked tree that, once the bright foliage has fallen, brings architectural interest to your garden, like the Autumn Brilliance Serviceberry tree, available from trees.com.

Attractively planted container gardens are a landscape designer's best friend during the cooler months of the year. These portable pots of color and texture can be positioned wherever you want, adding a welcoming touch to an entrance or a bright splash to a patio table.

There are all sorts of vibrant plants that love life in a patio pot and last from fall into winter for a wonderful cool-season display. The go-to formula is to combine a thriller (height), filler (body) and spiller (edging) to create a full, textured look. Good choices for fall and winter include compact evergreens or ornamental grasses to add the height element, pansies to fill out the body of the planter, and ivy to tumble over the edges. For a modern look, squeezing a container full of a single variety is an even easier option.

Dwarf pine trees are enjoying a surge in popularity with landscape designers, and no wonder. While in spring, summer and fall they provide a green, textural foil to colorful flowers and leaves, once winter arrives they emerge as standout sculptural shapes. The beauty of slow-growing dwarf pines is that they naturally stay compact and well-shaped, so need very little looking after.

Dwarf Mugo pine (Pinus mugo var. pumilo) is a particular favorite thanks to its dense, dark needles. While it eventually grows to 3-5 feet high, its easily kept pruned into a neat mound. Dwarf Mugo pine, available from trees.com, keeps its seed cones all through winter, adding to the display. Adding one, or an odd number dotted through your outside space, is a great way to ensure your garden has character throughout the year, and helps avoid that sensation of sparseness that often happens in winter when many plants are dormant.

When summer perennials and annuals fade you will need something else to take their place. Autumn-flowering bulbs will pop up reliably year after year to add color and, if you choose the right sunny spot, will naturalise, gently spreading to reflect how comfortable they are in your landscape. This repetition will strengthen the planting design, as well as adding continuity.

Nerine bowdenii (also known as as Bowden lily) brings an explosion of color to rejuvenate tired fall borders. These beautiful bright blooms on sturdy stems grow to 24 inches, and can be added to pots as well as planted in drifts through a flowerbed. In addition to the standard pink petals, there are varieties from white through to a deep ruby red, and Dutch Grown stocks a good nerine selection. While it's too late to plant the bulbs this year, you can pre-order ready for planting in spring for floral fireworks next fall.

Another favorite is hardy cyclamen, which you'll likely find potted at your local garden center in fall. Plant a mix of the hardy ivy-leaved cyclamen (Cyclamen hederifolium), which flowers in fall and has pretty foliage that lasts through winter, and winter-flowering cyclamen (Cyclamen coum) which blooms winter into spring, for a long season of interest.

Even during the cooler months of the year, your outdoor space should be an inviting spot where you want to linger. A fire pit creates an ambient spot to gather around at dusk offers the opportunity for year-round entertaining, and is a go-to for landscape designers interested in creating an outdoor space with year-round usability.

Whether you want the crackle of real wood burning or the convenience of starting a fire with the click of a button, a fire pit will add visual as well as physical warmth to your fall and winter garden. Opt for a design that complements your overall aesthetic and add layers of textured cushions and throws in a palette of warm, earthy tones to garden furniture to build on the cozy vibe.

This fire pit is perfect for patio evenings, bringing friends and family together around a warm, inviting flame. Though it burns real wood, an airflow system facilitates efficient combustion to reduce smoke levels.

Fuelled by wood or wood pellets, this 7-inch diameter stainless steel mini fire pit brings the s'mores experience to a patio table. Efficient burning means it's smokeless and a removable ash pan makes clean-up easy.

Fuelled by propane gas, this stylish fire pit has an adjustable flame and can deliver plenty of warmth to keep your outdoor gatherings cozy. A lid lets it double as a coffee table and it's also available in black and stone-look.

Landscape designers often use a mix of materials to maximize texture and interest in garden design. These are automatically thrown into the spotlight during the cooler months of the year when plants die back and leave the hardscaping exposed. Gravel offers a softer alternative to bare paving slabs, and buff-toned pea gravel in mixed sizes and shapes like this landscaping pea gravel from Lowe's creates a particularly natural look.

Softening the edges of hard landscaping with plants is another landscape-designer trick that comes into its own as a garden moves from fall into winter. Use evergreen creeping thyme to blend the edges of a curving pathway and a functional garden element becomes an attractive winter feature. You can also use gravel as a mulch to avoid areas of bare border soil in winter, an option that's also easy on the budget.

Another way to maximise texture and help your garden transition from fall into winter without missing a beat is to add warm lighting from a low angle. In fall, a ground-stake spotlight will highlight foliage but then is easily moved to throw a glow through ornamental grass blades. Even a wash of light across a gravel path creates a play of shadows that shows off texture to the full, and allows you to appreciate your garden from indoors on dark evenings.

The easiest way to create individual pools of low light is with solar spotlights, although you'll need to shop smart when buying outdoor solar lights that will generate enough power in fall and winter. For a start, choose lights with adjustable solar panels so they can be angled to match the sun's lower position in the sky. Look for a high conversion rate and a large capacity battery of at least 1500 mAh, too, and opt for warm-toned LEDS that will create more ambiance than bright white lights. These Solar Spot Lights from Wayfair tick all three boxes.

Lifestyle journalist Sarah Wilson writes about garden design and landscaping trends. She has studied introductory garden and landscape design, and also has an RHS Level 2 qualification in the Principles of Plant Growth and Development. She is a regular contributor to Homes & Gardens and Livingetc. She has also written for Country Living, Country Homes & Interiors, and Modern Gardens magazines.

Healthier Salt Substitute May Prevent Millions of Deaths From Heart Disease

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A simple swap for salt could spare millions of cardiovascular disease-related deaths, new research suggests.

In a study published in Nature Health on November 4, researchers at The George Institute for Global Health modeled the potential impact of 193 countries switching from regular table salt to potassium-enriched salt.

They found the switch could prevent nearly 3 million deaths and more than 10 million new cases of heart and kidney disease worldwide each year.

In the United States, this simple salt swap could prevent nearly 105,000 deaths and about 492,000 new cases a year, the researchers estimated.

About 130.6 million U.S. adults have cardiovascular disease, according to the American Heart Association (AHA).

Liping Huang, PhD, a senior data analyst in the Food Policy Division at The George Institute for Global Health and co-lead author of the study, told Healthline the team started with current blood pressure levels by sex, age, and country.

The researchers then estimated how replacing all salt with a salt substitute would change people’s sodium and potassium intake, blood pressure, and disease risk, she said.The team modeled a blend of 75% sodium chloride and 25% potassium chloride. The study estimated these yearly results worldwide:

The authors wrote that the model relied on data from a few years ago and on many assumptions, and that any large-scale switch would take years.

Potassium-enriched salt replaces some of the sodium chloride in regular table salt with potassium chloride, according to The George Institute.Asked where other salts like kosher salt and sea salt fit in, Huang said those salts may contain some trace elements “which has those health halos around them, but essentially they’re still primarily sodium chloride.”Huang noted that potassium-enriched salt tastes “about the same as a regular table salt.” She said it’s possible a small number of people with a sensitive palate could taste a difference.

Huang said that “it’s really hard for people to cut back their salt intake.”“But the easier way is just to replace part of the sodium chloride in our normal salt with potassium chloride, because increasing potassium intake also helps to reduce blood pressure,” she said.The two minerals play opposite roles in the body, according to Cheng-Han Chen, MD, a board certified interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA. Chen wasn’t involved in the study.

“While sodium causes water retention and increased blood pressure, potassium will help the kidneys remove sodium from the body and lower blood pressure,” Chen told Healthline.Huang said she worked on an earlier trial of about 21,000 people in 600 rural villages in northern China in 2021.

People who used potassium-enriched salt had a 14% lower risk of stroke, a 13% lower risk of cardiovascular events, and a 12% lower risk of early death than those who used regular salt, according to a 2021 study in The New England Journal of Medicine that summarized the trial’s results.

The World Health Organization (WHO) endorsed potassium-enriched salt in a 2025 guideline, saying it can reduce blood pressure and the risk of cardiovascular disease.

The AHA said potassium-based salt substitutes can help lower blood pressure in some adults. The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) issued labeling guidance in 2020 allowing an alternate name for potassium chloride on ingredient labels to include the word “salt.”“We think that blood pressure begins to improve as soon as 1 week after cutting back on sodium intake. The full benefit typically takes up to 4 weeks,” Chen said.

“We generally recommend a daily sodium limit of 2,300 milligrams (mg), ideally under 1,500 mg a day. Most people consume over 3,000 mg of sodium a day, well above the recommended limit,” Chen said.The WHO recommends less than 2,000 mg of sodium per day and suggests at least 3,510 mg of potassium a day.In the United States, more than 70% of salt intake comes from processed and restaurant foods, according to the AHA.

Huang said that some people with advanced chronic kidney disease should avoid potassium-enriched salt.When the kidneys don’t work well, the body may not clear extra potassium efficiently, she said. That can lead to hyperkalemia, or high potassium levels in the blood, which can raise the risk of cardiac arrest, Huang said.Among people with stage 3 to stage 5 kidney disease, switching to salt substitutes could prevent about 750,000 deaths a year by lowering blood pressure and cause about 100,000 deaths a year from hyperkalemia, the researchers estimated.The AHA said that people with kidney disease or who take medications that affect potassium should check with a healthcare professional before using these products.

An estimated 37 million U.S. adults have chronic kidney disease, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

Potassium-enriched salt is available at supermarkets in the U.S. Many major U.S. grocery chains, including Kroger, Walmart, and Publix, carry two types of products, according to their websites:

Potassium-enriched salt typically costs about twice as much as regular salt, according to the study.Huang said she hoped people would see the results and start thinking of the switch as “a simple thing I can do for their own health.”

Although it is still fairly early in the Championship season, many onlookers are now starting to see exactly why Middlesbrough were viewed as promotion material under Kim Hellberg in pre-season.

Middlesbrough's Swedish head coach has enjoyed a fine 12-month period at the Riverside Stadium, even if last season ended in heartbreak on two counts.

The style of play at the Riverside Stadium has been completely transformed within said timeframe, and across the opening three months of 2026/27, one man has finally solved the club's biggest achilles heel from last season's final weeks - the ability to score goals.

That is, of course, none other than Will Lankshear, who was one of several high-profile names to have moved to Teesside throughout the summer, doing so from Tottenham Hotspur.

The 21-year-old was no stranger to the Championship at the time of his initial £10m switch from the North London club, but his remarkable adjustment to life at the Riverside could be rewarded with the biggest compliment.

Spurs sold Will Lankshear to Boro in the summer, but, they have clauses written into the contract to protect their position in the future.

The aforementioned deal could still rise as high as £20m with add-ons, meaning Spurs can still benefit financially through their academy graduate's success.

However, right now, no one is benefiting more from Lankshear's goalscoring prowess and all-round game than Hellberg, with his recent double against Charlton Athletic taking the 21-year-old's tally to 13 goals in as many Championship games.

There were concerns over his fitness for Wednesday night's clash at Wrexham, but after being given the all-clear, it has been revealed that a senior England scout will oversee Lankshear's performance on Thomas Tuchel's behalf, with the Three Lions boss set to confirm his latest squad on Friday morning.

Such feats come less than two months after Lankshear marked his Under-21s debut with a goal in the 4-2 win over Kazakhstan at Molineux, further highlighting his remarkable short-term progress.

By mid-October, Lankshear's rapid ascent into double figures was only matched by Manchester City's Erling Haaland, and given his remarkable goalscoring feats, comparisons have already been drawn between him and the 2026 Ballon d'Or winner, Harry Kane, who also began his stellar career in North London.

"I can understand the comparisons with Harry Kane," claimed former Spurs and England goalkeeper, Paul Robinson. "He has headed down a similar career path to Harry and, physically, he has a similar stature as well.

"Like Kane, he looks like a player who is developing a little later, compared to others who are Premier League regulars before the end of their teens.

Spurs' recent fortunes have certainly been put under the microscope, and whilst Roberto De Zerbi's side have won their last three Premier League games, the Italian's attackers are still yet to fully step up to the plate.

Dominic Solanke, in fact, has only scored 24 goals in his first 76 appearances for the club, with one of those coming in the current campaign's opening weeks.

It was discussed at length whether Lankshear should have been retained at the Tottenham Hotspur Stadium after his 12-goal haul at Oxford United last season, and given the latest set of developments, De Zerbi may be having second thoughts over comments made over the transfer in September.

“Three strikers, how many strikers do you want in the squad? Three strikers I think are enough. To answer about the player in Middlesbrough, no (regrets)."

The Championship has been a breeding ground for homegrown talent in recent years, with many of Tuchel's England regulars starring in the second tier at some point of their careers.

However, they have, by and large, only gained national recognition since featuring regularly in one of Europe's major top-flights, but Lankshear could be about to buck such a trend.

Lionesses drawn with France, Austria and Portugal in 2027 Women’s Nations League

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

England will meet France, Austria and Portugal in the group stage of the 2027 UEFA Women’s Nations League after Tuesday’s draw at UEFA headquarters in Nyon. Sarina Wiegman’s side were placed in League A, Group 2, and will play each of their three opponents twice, once at home and once away, during the first half of next year.

The Football Association has confirmed that the group games will be spread across three international windows. Matchdays one and two fall between 23 February and 6 March 2027, matchdays three and four between 13 and 24 April, and the last two rounds between 7 and 15 June. Individual dates and venues are still to be announced.

This is the third edition of the competition, and once again it forms part of the qualifying route for major tournaments, this time Euro 2029 and the 2028 Olympic Games in Los Angeles. Only the four group winners in League A go through to the semi-finals, which are pencilled in for October 2027. The final and the third-place match follow between late November and early December.

None of the three opponents will be unfamiliar. England met Portugal in the previous Nations League campaign, France were their first opponents at Euro 2025, and Austria faced the Lionesses at Euro 2022 and again in World Cup qualifying shortly afterwards.

Wiegman, who agreed fresh terms running to 2029 in October, picked out France as the standout name in the group. “They’re a world-class team,” she told the FA’s official website. “They have so much quality, they have power, they have technique and they have some players, of course, also playing in the WSL and at other top clubs.”

She added: “So it’s exciting to play them because you will be challenged in the highest possible way and that’s what makes us better.”

The head coach also highlighted how adaptable Portugal can be. “They’re a team who can change their style from playing a possession game to being a little more direct,” she said. As for Austria, England have not played them since early 2024, and Wiegman sees a side on the way back up, noting that “now they’re finding the top level again and will definitely be difficult opponents across in both fixtures.”

The Nations League is still several months away. Before it begins, England have a two-legged World Cup qualifying play-off against Ukraine in December, with a ticket to next summer’s finals in Brazil up for grabs. “Of course, we want to qualify [for the World Cup] first when we play Ukraine in December,” Wiegman said.

For more on the national team, read our reports Bedford Keeps England Focused as U20 World Cup Uncertainty Grows and Nike Taps Williamson to Host NYC England Watch Party.

Scotland are also in the top tier and, as BBC Sport reports, will take on Germany, the Netherlands and Sweden in Group 4. The Republic of Ireland were drawn with Spain, Norway and Switzerland in Group 1, while Wales will face Denmark, Italy and Iceland in Group 3. Northern Ireland are in League B, where Poland, Finland and Belarus make up Group 3.

Safari finally gets 3 popular browser features – and I can’t wait to try them

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

New developer beta release notes point to deeper Siri AI integration and more Safari customization.

Developer betas for MacOS 27.3, iPadOS 27.3, and iOS 27.3 are here, with a few major upgrades to the Safari browser. Vertical tabs, adaptive dark mode, and deeper Siri AI integration are on the way to the public, but are first in beta for testing.

Also: Your Apple Watch has 2 new controversial listening features – but you have to opt in

Siri AI is expected to be further woven into Music, Messages, Photos, Calendar, and Safari. Adaptive dark mode will allow for any website to be viewed in dark mode, even if the site doesn’t support it. Finally, vertical tabs are pretty straightforward, enabling vertical navigation and drag-and-drop tab reorganization.

Here’s what you can expect when the software goes public in mid-December.

Also: 3 handy iPhone features that make my life easier – even doing laundry

Siri AI gains more access to your Apple devices, allowing more cross-app functionality and the ability to execute complex, multi-step tasks within your apps.

According to 9to5Mac, you can ask Siri AI to create a playlist in Music and save it to your library, request messages to be scheduled for a later send, organize and label photos, and create a detailed calendar invite — all with your voice.

The same reporting also states that Siri AI features will appear in Maps, Weather, Shortcuts, and Notes, signaling that an AI-supercharged Siri will live in every crevice of your Apple devices.

Also: iOS 27 will organize your open Safari tabs now

I’m not one to complete tasks like the ones mentioned above on my iPhone — I’m far more likely to do them on my MacBook Air, since I associate the laptop with productivity. Every blue moon I’ll rearrange my entire Apple Music catalog, every month I fill out my calendar, and sometimes I’ll go through Photos and try to organize it. I can see myself automating certain steps in these tasks, and I’m excited to try it.

A WebKit blog post details that adaptive dark mode is coming to Safari, how it will work, and what users can expect. According to the post, 40% of web page loads are from dark mode users, but only 10% of web pages natively support it.

I’m particularly excited about this feature, as I set my Apple devices to automatically enable dark mode at 7 p.m. to encourage a more relaxing evening scrolling vibe, especially while reading. I read a lot on my MacBook, and in a dark room, a bright page can feel too bright, but dimming my laptop’s display doesn’t quite do the trick.

Also: Your AirPods got better in iOS 27 – three new ways I’m using them

The WebKit blog post states that adaptive dark mode uses “a color matrix to invert the luminosity of colors while preserving hue” and distinguishes between when a page’s color is purely decorative and when it carries substantial meaning, to ensure the user experience remains consistent between light and dark modes.

Vertical browser tabs aren’t a new concept, but they’ll soon be new to Safari. I see the value in vertical tabs: a list view is easier to scan, web page titles aren’t smushed when several tabs are open, and they’re particularly useful when you’re working on a widescreen monitor.

Many people who spend hours using their Mac or MacBook may find this upcoming feature useful. I do believe that Safari is the superior browser for personal use, given its integration with your Apple devices, especially with features like Handoff.

Also: 7 AI-free iOS 27 features that make old iPhones exciting again

However, I do have one major gripe with vertical tabs: they’re usually locked to the left panel, and I prefer the right. If Safari allows this customization, I’ll seriously consider switching from horizontal tabs.

No One You Love Is Ever Dead: Hemingway on Loss and the Meaning of Life

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

Along the spectrum of losses, from the door keys to the love of one’s life, none is more unimaginable, more incomprehensible in its unnatural violation of being and time, than a parent’s loss of a child.

Ernest Hemingway (July 21, 1899–July 2, 1961) was in his twenties and living in France when he befriend Gerald and Sara Murphy. The couple eventually returned to America when one of their sons fell ill, but it was their other son, Baoth, who died after a savage struggle with meningitis.

Upon receiving the news, the thirty-five-year-old writer sent his friends an extraordinary letter, part consolation for and part consecration of a loss for which there is no salve, found in Shaun Usher’s moving compilation Letters of Note: Grief (public library).

You know there is nothing we can ever say or write… Yesterday I tried to write you and I couldn’t.

It is not as bad for Baoth because he had a fine time, always, and he has only done something now that we all must do. He has just gotten it over with…

About him having to die so young — Remember that he had a very fine time and having it a thousand times makes it no better. And he is spared from learning what sort of a place the world is.

It is your loss: more than it is his, so it is something that you can, legitimately, be brave about. But I can’t be brave about it and in all my heart I am sick for you both.

Absolutely truly and coldly in the head, though, I know that anyone who dies young after a happy childhood, and no one ever made a happier childhood than you made for your children, has won a great victory. We all have to look forward to death by defeat, our bodies gone, our world destroyed; but it is the same dying we must do, while he has gotten it all over with, his world all intact and the death only by accident.

In a breathtaking sentiment evocative of Anaïs Nin’s admonition against the stupor of near-living, and of poet Meghan O’Rourke’s grief-honed conviction that “the people we most love do become a physical part of us, ingrained in our synapses, in the pathways where memories are created,” Hemingway adds:

Very few people ever really are alive and those that are never die; no matter if they are gone. No one you love is ever dead.

With this, echoing Auden’s insistence that “we must love one another or die,” he comes the closest he ever came to formulating the meaning of life. Like David Foster Wallace, who addressed the meaning of life with such exquisite lucidity shortly before he was slain by depression, Hemingway too would lose hold of that meaning in the throes of the agony that would take his life a quarter century later. Now, from the fortunate platform of the prime of life, he writes:

We must live it, now, a day at a time and be very careful not to hurt each other. It seems as though we were all on a boat together, a good boat still, that we have made but that we know will never reach port. There will be all kinds of weather, good and bad, and especially because we know now that there will be no landfall we must keep the boat up very well and be very good to each other. We are fortunate we have good people on the boat.

Complement with the young Dostoyevsky’s exultation about the meaning of life shortly after his death sentence was repealed, Emily Dickinson on love and loss, Thoreau on living through loss, and Nick Cave — who lived, twice, the unimaginable tragedy of the Murphys — on grief as a portal to aliveness, then revisit the fascinating neuroscience of your brain on grief and your heart on healing.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. Details, Video of Jugal II First Ascent

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The Spanish team is back in Kathmandu after their first ascent of Jugal II via a highly difficult 2,000m route. Yet, the climbers insist that the best part of the experience was climbing part of the route with Italians Nives Meroi and Romano Benet. Jugal II was Plan B for the Italian pair after flooding washed out access to their primary target, Tilicho Peak in the Annapurna region.

The peak, northeast of Kathmandu on the Nepal-Tibet border, has been a coveted goal since it opened to climbing teams in 2023. In the end, it was the skill and smart strategy of Mikel Zabalza, Ruben Perez, and Mikel Saez de Urabain that put them on the unclimbed 6,518m summit after an alpine-style push via the east-southeast face.

Route topo, including a special mention of Nives Meroi and Romano Benet.

The team said that the greatest technical difficulties came high on the route. “From the upper bivouac, the climb goes on pretty vertical mixed terrain, with sections of poor-quality rock,” Zabalza explained. “It’s sustained M5 with some even harder sections, until 6,450m. Then the rest of the way to the summit follows along a 55˚-60˚snow slope.”

A tough, vertical mixed section. Photo: Mikel Zabalza

They reached the summit on October 29, at 4:00 pm. The weather was clear but very cold, with some evening humidity.

“We made three bivouacs on the route, two on the way up and one more on descent, and all of them were rather precarious,” Mikel Zabalza told ExplorersWeb from Kathmandu. “Luckily, we had a tarp to enlarge them.”

One of the climbers on the summit of Jugal II. Photo: Mikel Zabalza

The Spanish team, all highly experienced alpinists, had been studying the route for months. This was key to their success.

The situation was different for Benet and Meroi of Italy. Since they had not planned to do Jugal II, their approach to the route was more improvisational. They had no previous information about what they would find. To make matters worse, the third member of the team, Tine Cuder of Slovenia, had to abandon the expedition due to health problems.

One of the Spanish team’s bivouacs, with a tiny ledge enlarged by a fabric tarp. Photo: Romano Benet

There was no competition between the two teams, who happily joined forces for the summit push. However, the Spaniards’ strategy proved essential on the highly steep ramps covered with unstable snow.

Nivel Meroi loaded with bivy gear on highly steep mixed ramps on Jugal II. Photo: Romano Benet

“We set off on an adventure, having never seen the mountain before and not knowing what kind of ascent awaited us, but we gave it a try,” Romano Benet reported from Kathmandu. “We decided to turn back to avoid a third bivouac. ”

The two teams joined forces and were in good spirits on the route. Photo: Romano Benet

Benet explained that they lacked the necessary gear (specifically, a tarp like Zabalza’s group) to pitch bivvies “on this type of face, where it’s impossible to carve out a ledge.”

Otherwise, the two groups returned as friends. “Nives and Romano are exceptional people,” Zabalza said.

“We are back from the mountain with new experiences and ideas but, most of all, with three new fantastic friends with whom, hopefully, we will share more adventures in the future,” Romano Benet concluded.

The Spanish team sent us the video below summarizing the climb.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

French Creek Workshops House / Wittman Estes

Sydney's new $641m Powerhouse arts and science museum to open amid crisis

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The two-wing museum project is counting down to a grand opening Courtesy Powerhouse Studio

Sydney’s Powerhouse, a new arts, design, science and technology museum that the New South Wales government describes as its biggest cultural investment since the city’s Opera House, is due to open on 7 November amid controversy and uncertainty.

The most recent crisis is the departure of Lisa Havilah, the museum’s chief executive, who stepped down in August over allegations of misconduct. Havilah had been the figurehead of the Powerhouse development at Parramatta, located in Sydney’s multicultural western suburbs, since 2019.

Some of Australia’s most powerful and influential arts identities have since signed a petition calling for Havilah to be reinstated immediately. The petition was launched on 27 October by Kon Gouriotis, the editor of Artist Profile magazine.

Among the more than 750 signatories so far are the prominent artist Ben Quilty, the architect and arts patron Penelope Seidler, the commercial gallery owner Anna Schwartz, and Lucy Turnbull, the former lord mayor of Sydney (2003-04) whose husband Malcolm Turnbull was Australia's prime minister (2015-18).

John Graham, the state’s arts minister, also announced that Powerhouse’s chief operating officer, Mark Wilsdon, is on leave while a Sydney law firm, Lander & Rogers, investigates the allegations. Those allegations have not been publicly detailed, but Graham has said they were brought forward by a whistleblower and touch on “governance and procurement”. Graham’s office did not respond before we went to press to a question about whether the inquiry’s findings will be made public.

Powerhouse is part of the broader Museum of Applied Arts and Sciences, which also encompasses a large site in Ultimo, a storage area and discovery centre in Castle Hill, and the Sydney Observatory.

The Ultimo site was the location of the Powerhouse Museum and its half a million objects from 1893 until the state government decided to distribute the city’s cultural assets more equitably across the population base. In 2015, Parramatta, about 20km west of central Sydney, was selected as the new site.

The Ultimo building was closed in 2024 and is now undergoing a A$300m ($210m) heritage revitalisation by the state government.

But a public petition has been launched to save the site following a newspaper report that the government is secretly considering selling part of the Ultimo site to cover the cost of the new Powerhouse.

The closure of the Ultimo site is a cultural “tragedy”, heritage expert and former Powerhouse trustee Kylie Winkworth tells The Art Newspaper. “It’s a globally unprecedented museum demolition,” explains the vocal member of the Powerhouse Museum Alliance pressure group. Located on the banks of the Parramatta River, the new Powerhouse was designed by the Franco-Japanese architectural practice Moreau Kusunoki in collaboration with the Australian firm Genton. Its exhibition spaces cover more than 18,000 sq. m across seven vast, column-free galleries. It also has a rooftop terrace and garden, and temporary accommodation for students, artists and scientists.

Dubbed by some “the milk crate” because of its steel criss-cross design, the Powerhouse is in good company in earning an irreverent nickname: the Sydney Opera House was called a “scrum of nuns” in the 1970s because its sails resembled wimples. While the Opera House was made a Unesco World Heritage Site in 2007, a similarly illustrious future for the Powerhouse, which cost A$915m ($641m) to build, is far from assured.

Even before construction began, experts told a parliamentary inquiry that the museum’s riverfront undercroft would flood in heavy rain. This led to changes in the building’s design.

During a tour around the site in June, before her departure, Havilah told The Art Newspaper that “the whole of Parramatta would be under water” before the Powerhouse flooded. However, Winkworth says she fears people within the building would still be at risk in the event of overland flooding.

Already widely viewed as having consumed a disproportionate share of the state’s arts budget, the Powerhouse has in recent months been the focus of a new funding scandal—the backdrop to Havilah’s departure. The Sydney Morning Herald reported in August that more than A$70m ($46.2m) had been spent in the run-up to the museum’s grand opening, including A$10m ($7m) for LED screens, staging and backdrop for an immersive experience created by the British artist Es Devlin.

Another point of contention was Havilah’s introduction of a five-year “artistic associates” programme, costing A$2.6m ($1.8m), in which external experts were employed to help bring the Powerhouse to fruition. The Public Service Association, a trade union, claimed this sidelined long-serving and knowledgeable Powerhouse curators in favour of outsider non-specialists. The programme was scrapped in May following a review by the state treasury.

After Havilah’s departure, Abbie Galvin, the New South Wales government’s architect, took over as interim chief executive. At the same time, the state appointed former Sydney Opera House CEO Louise Herron as a “strategic adviser”. Cassandra Wilkinson, a New South Wales treasury executive, took over Wilsdon’s role in July, in an environment of constant media reports about apparent cost blowouts in the lead-up to the launch.

Campaigners are unhappy that key treasures of the Powerhouse collection will not be on display in the new building. The 1785 Boulton & Watt steam engine, the 1855 Locomotive No.1 and the Strasburg Clock model—along with most of the rest of the collection—are stored off-site in a discovery centre that is open to the public only on weekends.

Winkworth says the Ultimo site was “uniquely resonant and appropriate for the display of the Powerhouse Museum’s internationally significant power and transport collections. Now the collections are dismembered at Castle Hill and never to be seen together again.”

She argues that the new Powerhouse was “never intended to be a museum” at all but rather a function and performance centre, helping boost the local nighttime economy.

Five inaugural shows are all scheduled to open on 7 November. Task Eternal, the main draw, traces the history of aviation and space exploration in partnership with the Smithsonian and the British Museum. Es Devlin: The Turning features five major works by the artist and stage designer. The Dark, for children, turns darkness into a “playground of curiosity” through interactive storytelling, a crystal tunnel and 100 borrowed childhood toys.

The fourth show, Overflow, explores the care of salt and freshwater ecosystems around Australia. Finally, The Mall centres on western Sydney’s pioneering role in the global development of shopping malls as part of modern culture and lifestyle.

General admission to the Powerhouse will be free. Over-16s will pay A$30 ($21) for a ticket to Task Eternal. All visitors will pay from A$10 ($7) for James Turrell’s light-based installation, Shangri La (Over the Hump). A 23-day opening festival will feature 179 events including Space Shuttle astronaut Charles Bolden reflecting on leadership in space exploration. Most of the opening events are free.

NSW government scraps controversial billion-dollar relocation plan but faces questions from community groups and parliament

Lisa Havilah had stood aside pending “investigations into governance and procurement”, according to New South Wales’ arts minister

The Art Gallery of New South Wales has been expanded with a new building by Japanese architects SANAA, while new displays will push Australia's Indigenous artists to the fore

Deathbed portrait of Vanessa Bell by Duncan Grant discovered

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Duncan Grant, Portrait of Vanessa Bell (1961)

A poignant portrait of the artist Vanessa Bell, made within days of her death in 1961 by her sometime lover and lifelong friend Duncan Grant, has been rediscovered among family papers and donated to the famous home of the Bloomsbury Group, Charleston farmhouse in East Sussex.

The drawing in soft pencil and biro, from an angle suggesting Grant was sitting by her bedside, was rediscovered among family papers during research for a forthcoming exhibition of the work of both artists at Tate Britain. It may have been made just after Bell’s death, though she could just be resting peacefully with eyes closed.

Although Grant has dated the drawing “Friday April 14”, Bell actually died at the farmhouse on a week prior. In a statement from Charleston, now a museum and archive, Darren Clarke, head of collections and research, says that in later years, Grant and his friends went through masses of the work in his studio identifying and labelling pictures, so the date may have been added in error years later.

“What seems very likely here is that Duncan knew this drawing had been made around Vanessa’s death and later added a date from memory. He got remarkably close, but apparently not quite close enough. Everything about the drawing itself – its viewpoint, its intimacy and its extraordinary economy – suggests someone sitting quietly beside Vanessa’s bed and drawing the face he had known for half a century.”

The friendship and artistic partnership of Grant and Bell was one of the happiest and longest lasting of the tangled affairs of the group. Although Grant was gay and a former lover of Bell’s brother Adrian, they were lovers for a time and in 1918 had a child, Angelica—who went on to marry David Garnett, another of Grant’s lovers. Grant and Bell lived and worked at the 17th-century farmhouse for decades, covering many of the interiors with striking painted decoration. He outlived her by 17 years, and in 1978 was buried beside her in the churchyard of St Peter’s in Firle, near their home.

Grant was long regarded as the most talented artist of the group, and the first major solo exhibition of Bell’s work came only in 2017 at the Dulwich Picture Gallery. At that time most of her paintings in the Tate collection were languishing in storage, but her reputation has been growing steadily in recent years. The Tate is making amends with a major exhibition of work by both artists, Vanessa Bell and Duncan Grant: Inside Bloomsbury (12 November 2026 - 11 April 2027) at Tate Britain.

Although Charleston is the largest lender to the Tate exhibition, including the entire contents of Grant’s studio, the drawing will only be included in the exhibition catalogue. It will go on public display for the first time at Charleston in May 2027, when the studio reopens after major conservation work.

The drawing has been donated to Charleston as part of the 50 for 50 campaign which aims to bring 50 major works by the group back to the farmhouse in time for 2030, the 50th anniversary of the founding of the Charleston Trust, which runs and maintains the museum.

Nathaniel Hepburn, the director and CEO of Charleston, says in a statement on the discovery: “I found this drawing deeply moving when I first saw it. We know Duncan’s drawings of Vanessa so intimately, made over the course of their long friendship, that the discovery of a previously unknown work is remarkable in itself. But there is something incredibly poignant about imagining Duncan sitting beside Vanessa, drawing her after her death.“

A new book and UK exhibition by Charlie Porter explores the sartorial choices of Virginia Woolf, Vanessa Bell and the rest of the Charleston set

British artist Hew Locke has been selected for Tate Britain’s Duveen Galleries commission, while Barbara Hepworth gets a survey at Tate St Ives

NASA Navigation Experiment Shows Promise for Planetary Landings

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

NASA successfully demonstrated technology designed to help spacecraft autonomously navigate to safe landing sites on the Moon and on other planets.

An Alta-X drone equipped with the agency’s Safe and Precise Landing – Integrated Capabilities Evolution (SPLICE) guidance, navigation, and control payload completed simulated lunar descent and landing maneuvers Aug. 27. The flight took place at Rogers Dry Lake near NASA’s Armstrong Flight Research Center in Edwards, California.

During the tests, the SPLICE payload used an advanced guidance system to map the terrain and assess potential landing areas. If the system determines the targeted landing area is unsafe, the system diverts the drone to a safer location. The system generates high-resolution digital elevation maps from light-detection and sensor data.

Researchers at NASA’s Johnson Space Center in Houston developed the SPLICE payload and partnered with specialists at NASA Armstrong to integrate, test, and fly it aboard the Alta-X drone in June and July. Those tests verified communications, data handling, telemetry, and command- control performance between the SPLICE payload and the drone to prepare for a full-scale test at Voyager Technologies in Mojave, California, later this month.

“This flight test is the culmination of almost a decade of work, for the SPLICE project,” said Davis Adams, NASA project manager. “This flight raised the technology readiness level, priming it for integration into future robotic and crewed lunar flights. Validation of the guidance and navigation systems are applicable to terrestrial, lunar, and Martian descent and landing missions.”

NASA has worked with a number of commercial lunar lander providers to implement iterations of SPLICE into their platforms. A number of the landers slated for upcoming Moon Base missions leverage data collected by NASA through the development of systems like SPLICE to execute soft touchdowns on the lunar surface.

“We are coordinating with industry regarding the usage of the guidance system once the system is available in the NASA software catalog, currently planned for this fall,” Adams said. “Some of the companies also are  evaluating the system’s navigation capabilities for precision landing applications, as well as potential extensions for autonomous surface mobility.”

NASA’s Flight Opportunities project, based at NASA Armstrong, enabled flight testing of the SPLICE project early in its development that laid a foundation for it to continue advancing its technology readiness level to what it is today. Flight testing included both a descent altitude and a landing trajectory in 2019 similar to what is expected on a lunar mission and the demonstration of two NASA-developed precision landing sensor systems in 2020.

Research and Technology Mission Directorate

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

How much West Ham paid to sack Nuno Espirito Santo after missing the chance to do it for free

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

West Ham made the decision to sack Nuno Espirito Santo on Monday, after a 3-1 home defeat to West Brom. WHUFC have acted quickly to hire Scott Parker as their new boss, but they had to pay a sizeable compensation fee to part ways with Nuno.

The Athletic states that the Hammers paid around £2m to relieve the Portuguese boss of his duties. This is after having the option to do so for nothing after their relegation from the Premier League was confirmed in May.

New manager Parker had the support of board members as early as May, before opting to stick by Nuno. Mark Noble was among the 46-year-old’s strongest backers but Dutch coach John Heitinga was also considered as a potential replacement.

As soon as the decision was made to stick by Nuno in the summer, the pressure was on for the West Ham boss. He failed to keep them in the Premier League, but there was hope he could get them back there having led Wolves to Championship promotion in impressive fashion in 2017/18.

There were encouraging signs, as the Hammers rose as high as top of the table during a run of 10 Championship games unbeaten. They scored for fun too, scoring 30 times in the first 11 matches, including big wins over Wrexham, Lincoln City (both 6-0), Derby County (3-0) and Wolves (4-2).

However, frustrations over the style of football continued with cautious play in possession persisting despite WHUFC’s star power in attack. Defensive concerns persisted too, and when the warning signs led to defeats against Southampton and west Ham, the crowd had turned on Nuno.

United are ultimately made to pay for sticking by Nuno in the summer – £2m to be exact. The appointment of Parker for nothing limits the expense of this managerial change, but when the Portuguese boss could have gone in the summer for nothing, it has proven to be a costly decision by the club.

West Ham’s appointment of Parker has led to a mixed response. Many are pleased to see a former Hammers star and three-time Championship promotion-winning manager join the club, but there are some reservations over his style of play.

Like Nuno, Parker’s tactics in recent jobs have been built on a solid defensive foundation, showing a willingness to let the opposition have the ball, and when his sides do have it, playing conservatively.

However, Parker has adapted his tactics over time. While in charge of Fulham, his side had the second-highest average possession in the Championship with 60.8% (Fotmob), and they went on to win the league. They also recorded the highest xG (90.4 – 20 more than any other team), most big chances created (136) and most touches in the opposition box (1,352).

It will be intriguing to see if Parker reverts to this, or sticks by the defence-first tactics that saw him win promotion with Burnley after conceding only 16 goals in 46 matches.

WHUFC supporters want promotion at any cost, but the criticisms of Nuno’s style of play suggest they would rather brings more forward-thinking and controlling football.

Crunchbase Data: Less Than 2% Of 2026 Venture Funding Went To Post-Election Red States

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For the past few decades, the vast majority of U.S. startup funding has consistently gone to companies in blue states or purple states, where Democrats control all or some statewide offices.

But with Tuesday’s election results, the investment divide between blue, purple and red states has grown even more pronounced.

So far this year, less than 2% of all startup investment has gone to companies in post-election red states, defined as those that will have both a Republican governor and two Republican U.S. senators.

The red states’ share is much smaller than in years’ past due to five states shifting to purple. Of those, the largest venture hub is Texas, which shifted to purple with the election of Democratic Senator James Talarico and Governor Gina Hinojosa, followed by Ohio.

This year, as of Election Day, startups in the remaining 17 red states 1, which are home to nearly a quarter of Americans, had collectively pulled in just $8.6 billion in venture funding, with Florida, Tennessee and Utah among the largest contributors. To put that in perspective: It’s less than the typical nationwide total in a single week.

The 10 purple U.S. states2, meanwhile, are home to just over a quarter of Americans and pulled in about $28.6 billion this year as of Election Day. That’s much higher than the red state tally, but still less than 6% of all U.S. startup funding.

Beyond the aforementioned newcomers Texas and Ohio, several other purple states regularly pull in sizable venture funding, including Pennsylvania, North Carolina and to some extent Wisconsin.

Also worth noting: The divide between red, purple and blue states is somewhat less stark when it comes to round counts. About 12% of startup financings of $1 million or more went to purple state companies this year, and about 9% went to red states.

Broadly, it looks like backers are still finding plenty of investment-worthy talent outside of blue states when it comes to smaller deals. However, they are concentrating most of their capital into big AI rounds for California and New York companies.

This brings us to our next point: Blue states receive the overwhelming majority of startup funding, of which most goes to California.3 Per a Crunchbase analysis last week, California companies had pulled in more than three-fourths of all U.S. startup funding in 2026. Most of that stems from mega-financings for San Francisco-based OpenAI and Anthropic. While no other state comes close to California for attracting investment, the nearest runners up are also predominantly blue states, including New York and Massachusetts. In total, blue states have pulled in a staggering $481 billion in startup investment this year as of Election Day, per Crunchbase data, a new record. Politics and what makes for a venture hub So is it politics, or coincidence? Broadly speaking, the qualities that geographic startup hubs share — such as a deep tech talent pool, urban environs, leading tech companies and top research universities — aren’t specifically the domain of blue states. That said, predominantly rural states, which tend to lean red, are less likely to have those ingredients. Even so, there’s no rule against red state startup prowess. Austin, for example, managed to cement its status as a major tech hub even while Texas remained solidly under Republican control. And Utah also boasts a sophisticated startup scene. But following a Democratic electoral sweep, it’s hardly a surprise that more startup hubs now find themselves located in blue or purple states. JetBlue Raises Baggage Fees Again, Now Charges Up To $140 Roundtrip For One Checked Bag

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

JetBlue has raised checked baggage fees again, and bringing one ordinary suitcase can now add as much as $140 to a roundtrip ticket. With holiday travel approaching, this is another reminder that the fare you see when shopping may be far removed from what your trip actually costs.

JetBlue has increased baggage charges for new bookings made on or after November 3, 2026. The airline’s updated fee schedule lists a first checked bag starting at $50 each way. The price depends on when you travel and when you pay.

For passengers without an included baggage allowance or waiver, these are the new charges within the United States, Latin America, the Caribbean, and Canada. All amounts are per bag, each way:

You can see more particulars here, including pricing for Mosaic elites.

Upcoming peak periods include November 19–January 18. If you booked before November 3, check your reservation for the applicable charges (old rates apply). Here’s the full chart of peak dates over the next year:

That means one suitcase costs $100 roundtrip even at the lowest new rate. On peak dates, it costs $110 when paid in advance or $140 when purchased within 24 hours of each departure.

Consider a family of four traveling over Thanksgiving, with each person checking one suitcase and no baggage benefits.

Pay sufficiently early and the roundtrip baggage bill is $440. Wait until check-in for both flights and it becomes $560.

When does it become cheaper just to buy new clothes at your destination? We certainly try to pack efficiently when traveling with our children, but one standard checked bag per person hardly strikes me as an extravagant amount of luggage, especially for a longer holiday trip.

At these prices, baggage can materially change which airline offers the best deal. A cheaper JetBlue ticket may cease to be cheaper once you add the luggage you actually need…though it would not surprise me to see others follow.

Mosaic 1 members receive one checked bag, while Mosaic 2–4 members receive two. Eligible JetBlue Plus and Premier cardholders receive one bag. Premium-cabin allowances depend on the fare purchased: Base fares include one bag, while standard and Flex premium fares include two.

Those benefits become more valuable every time the underlying fees rise, though it’s still not a loyalty program or credit card product I can get enthusiastic about.

JetBlue’s latest increase pushes the cost of one checked suitcase to $100–$140 roundtrip for passengers without an exemption. For families, the additional expense can reach hundreds of dollars.

Compare the total cost before booking, and if you know you will check a bag, pay more than 24 hours before departure. I want JetBlue to succeed, but charging passengers more to bring their belongings does little to make it an airline people actively want to choose. Quote the contrary, JetBlue is doing a great job of alienating passengers during its great time of need.

CVS to split off drugstore business in major breakup of healthcare giant; shares fall 10%

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

CVS Health plans to split into two separate public companies by the first half 2028 in what would be one of the largest corporate breakups in history, the company announced Wednesday.

The move would reverse the company's massive $78 billion acquisition of its insurer, Aetna, and dismantle the vertically integrated healthcare conglomerate it created in 2018. The plan would specifically separate CVS' roughly 9,000 retail pharmacies from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark, one of the nation's three largest PBMs.

The decision follows a "comprehensive strategic review" by the company's board of directors, according to a news release. Shares of CVS closed more than 12% lower on Wednesday.

It's a stunning reversal for the healthcare powerhouse, which has spent the last two decades working to turn itself into a one-stop health destination. The move also comes as CVS' aggressive turnaround plan under CEO David Joyner shows substantial progress, with the company pursuing $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shake-ups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

But Joyner told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that the decision to split is "out of a position of strength," pointing to recovery in Aetna's Medicare business and solid performance in the retail pharmacy.

"So it's all about the strength of the business that affords us the opportunity to make what we think are the right strategic decisions for the future of this company," he later added.

CVS shares have jumped roughly 70% since Joyner took the helm in October 2024, bringing the company's market value to $117 billion.

The two new companies would both be publicly traded.

The insurance and PBM company will retain the CVS Health name and be run by Joyner, while the other company will be called CVS Pharmacy and be led by Prem Shah, who is currently group president of CVS Health. Additional leadership positions and board members will be announced at a later date, CVS said.

Existing CVS shareholders will own stock in both companies after the split. The Financial Times on Tuesday first reported the breakup plans.

The company's pharmacy business raked in $37.66 billion in 2025 sales, while its insurance unit booked $36.29 billion in revenue and the healthcare services segment generated $51.24 billion.

CVS on Wednesday also posted third-quarter earnings and revenue that topped Wall Street expectations. Total sales climbed to $108.40 billion, from $102.87 billion in the prior-year period.

Sales were boosted by year-over-year growth in the company's healthcare benefits and health services segments. Meanwhile, revenue in its pharmacy services business slid from the previous year.

On Wednesday, CVS said it will lump its insurer and PBM with its primary care business Oak Street Health, home visit provider Signify Health and the company's specialty drug business.

CVS also said the split will allow the two companies to experience a "focused strategic agenda" and greater ability to "innovate, adopt technology, increase efficiency," among other benefits.

It's not the first time CVS has weighed splitting the company. In October 2024, CVS engaged advisors in a strategic review of its business and considered breaking up its retail pharmacy and insurance units.

But Joyner told CNBC that the company was "in a turnaround" at that time without the flexibility, cash position or business performance to execute a split.

In an interview, Leerink Partners analyst Michael Cherny said the split shows "the stability of the pharmacy," which has won market share from competitors and is seeing gross margins stabilize thanks to CostVantage, a new pharmacy reimbursement model introduced by CVS last year that the company bills as more transparent.

"This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome," he told CNBC on Wednesday.

One potential concern for investors is whether the retail pharmacy will lose its competitive advantage of being directly linked with a PBM, which negotiates drug prices and manages prescription drug benefits on behalf of insurers and employers.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses "are going to be preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Cherny said the rationale for the split could also extend to the state legislative scrutiny over the company's level of vertical integration.

The potential breakup also comes as pharmacy benefit managers face mounting bipartisan scrutiny in Washington, D.C., and across states. Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban co-ownership of PBMs and pharmacies. Those have been challenged in court by CVS and other companies.

PBMs have been criticized for opaque pricing practices, their growing market power and potential conflicts of interest when they are owned by companies that also operate insurers and pharmacies.

« Une décision inattendue, que l’OL regrette profondément » : Le communiqué amer de l'Olympique Lyonnais officialisant le départ de Paulo Fonseca

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ce mercredi 4 novembre 2026, l'Olympique Lyonnais a officialisé le départ de Paulo Fonseca de ses fonctions d'entraîneur de l'équipe professionnelle vers le Sporting Portugal. Dans un communiqué au ton particulièrement piquant, la direction rhodanienne a fustigé le timing de cette décision inattendue et l'engagement du technicien.

La nouvelle a surpris tout le monde à Lyon. Alors que son contrat avec l'OL courait initialement jusqu'au 30 juin 2027, Paulo Fonseca a lui-même informé ses dirigeants de sa volonté de partir. Le technicien de 53 ans a en effet été approché ces derniers jours par le Sporting Portugal, un club auquel il est particulièrement attaché. Pour racheter son contrat, la formation portugaise versera d'ailleurs une indemnité de 5 millions d'euros à l'OL. Toutefois, cette indemnité ne console pas les décideurs lyonnais.

Arrivé le 31 janvier 2025, Paulo Fonseca a dirigé son 83e et dernier match dimanche 1er novembre 2026 lors de la victoire contre Angers (2-0) au Groupama Stadium. Il quitte Lyon en laissant le club à la 3e place de Ligue 1 avec 17 points, pour un bilan de 5 victoires, 2 nuls et 2 défaites. Une dynamique sportive positive qui rend la séparation d'autant plus amère pour l'OL.

Au-delà de la surprise créée par cette demande, ce sont surtout les termes choisis par l'OL dans son communiqué qui détonnent. Loin des formules polies d'usage, l'état-major lyonnais a pointé du doigt l'attitude de son entraîneur. La direction a ainsi estimé qu'il « n'était pas dans l'intérêt du club de poursuivre une collaboration qui ne pouvait plus garantir le niveau d'engagement total indispensable à la réussite du projet ». Un reproche cinglant adressé au Portugais.

En effet, le club rhodanien a insisté sur le fait que dans « une période aussi déterminante de la saison », l'ensemble des forces devait être « entièrement mobilisé et concentré sur les objectifs ». Face à un entraîneur désireux d'ailleurs, l'OL a préféré trancher immédiatement dans le vif. La direction s'active désormais pour identifier un successeur au profil capable de s'inscrire dans la continuité du projet sportif et des principes développés ces derniers mois.

Dans l'immédiat, l'Olympique Lyonnais a dû réagir rapidement pour gérer le quotidien. C'est Jorge Maciel qui a été désigné pour assurer l'intérim à la tête de l'équipe professionnelle jusqu'à la prochaine trêve internationale de novembre 2026. Le technicien va devoir prendre le relais sans temps d'adaptation avant deux déplacements importants.

Jorge Maciel dirigera ainsi les Gones face à la Real Sociedad ce jeudi 5 novembre 2026 à 18h45 au stade d'Anoeta, dans le cadre de la Ligue Europa. Dans la foulée, le technicien conduira également le groupe lyonnais lors du déplacement en Ligue 1 à Brest, prévu le dimanche 8 novembre 2026 à 17h15.

Southern Taurid meteor shower could bring fireballs ‪this week —‬ when and where to look

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The best time to see meteors during the Southern Taurid meteor shower is coming up soon.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

One of autumn's best opportunities to see brilliant "fireballs" arrives overnight Wednesday and Thursday (Nov. 4-5) during the Southern Taurid meteor shower.

Although the Southern Taurids are a minor meteor shower that produces only a handful of "shooting stars" each hour, it occasionally features exceptionally bright meteors called fireballs, which can briefly light up the night sky. According to NASA, fireballs are meteors at least as bright as Venus.

The Southern Taurids are active for several weeks in fall and peak Oct. 14, but the American Meteor Society expects the "fireball swarm component" to peak around Nov. 4-5.

Meteors can streak across any part of the sky, but for this meteor shower, they appear to radiate from the constellation Taurus, which will rise in the eastern sky as night falls and climb higher as the night progresses. That makes the hours around midnight a good time to look for shooting stars. However, unlike major meteor showers such as the Perseids (which peak Aug. 12-13) and the Geminids (which peak Dec. 13-14), the Southern Taurids have a broad peak, so there's no need to be outside at an exact time.

The Southern Taurids are the first of two fireball-rich meteor showers to be visible this month. The Northern Taurids will peak overnight on Nov. 11-12, giving stargazers another chance to spot a fireball. Both meteor showers result from dust and debris left in the inner solar system by Comet 2P/Encke striking Earth's atmosphere.

Discovered in 1786, Encke is a massive comet, measuring 2.98 miles (4.8 kilometers) in diameter, according to NASA ‪—‬ around one-third the size of the object thought to have killed the nonavian dinosaurs. Encke orbits the sun every 3.3 years, one of the shortest known orbital periods of any comet in the solar system. Scientists theorize that Encke is one of many fragments of a giant comet that broke apart 20,000 to 30,000 years ago.

After the Southern and Northern Taurids, the next meteor shower will be the Leonids, which peak Nov. 17-18 and produce around 15 shooting stars per hour, and the Geminids, which peak Dec. 13-14 and can deliver up to 120 shooting stars per hour close to the peak.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

It's best to watch meteor showers with the naked eye, but a good backyard telescope will serve you well in the months to come. As the long nights of winter kick off in the Northern Hemisphere, skywatching opportunities abound — including four consecutive supermoons from Nov. 2026 to Feb. 2027.

Jamie Carter is a Cardiff, U.K.-based freelance science journalist and a regular contributor to Live Science. He is the author of A Stargazing Program For Beginners and co-author of The Eclipse Effect, and leads international stargazing and eclipse-chasing tours. His work appears regularly in Space.com, Forbes, New Scientist, BBC Sky at Night, Sky & Telescope, and other major science and astronomy publications. He is also the editor of WhenIsTheNextEclipse.com.

OEB Architects channels 1980s interiors for London extension

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Glossy ceramic tiles, a glass-block wall and a leafy garden were intended to evoke tropical modernism and 1980s interiors for London Tropical, an extension to a Victorian terrace designed by local practice OEB Architects.

OEB Architects was tasked with creating an open-plan kitchen and dining space at the rear of the three-storey terrace in Southwark that would feel “surrounded” by its garden.

“The project considers how an extension to a typical Victorian outrigger can connect to its modest urban garden,” Gabriel George, project architect at the studio, told Dezeen.

“It explores the layering and overlaying of translucent and reflective materials and bold, graphic objects to give a nuanced and varied space through all seasons, contributing to a feeling of living surrounded by the garden,” he added.

Instead of filling the width of the site, OEB Architects pulled the extension back from the southern boundary, allowing it to be lined with both a glass-block wall and full-height windows that address a courtyard and side return.

This openness continues around to an end wall with full-height sliding glass doors, not only framing the garden but allowing the space to be entirely opened up to a patio surrounded by leafy planting.

“The clients were keen not to have a conventional top-lit infill extension, with a roof light sloping down to the boundary, as is common among extensions to Victorian terraces,” George told Dezeen.

“This expansion is set back from the boundary, providing both a passage and a new outlook from inside,” he continued.

“This results in two new external spaces, a courtyard addressing the main house and the rear garden serving the extension. Each is wrapped in full-height glazing with a range of transparencies responding to each view.”

The extension’s interior is defined by a kitchen island clad in glossy green tiles and a column clad in shiny red tiles, acting as spatial anchors that contrast the otherwise minimalist, white-painted surfaces, stone floors and pale timber carpentry and ceilings.

Both the glossy tiles and translucent glass-block wall were used to create a changing quality of light throughout the day, with the south-facing courtyard able to be screened off via translucent curtains that further add to this effect.

“The material and spatial ideas were driven by conversations with the clients discussing architectural precedents along with art, graphic and design references, including mid-century West Coast American case study houses, 80s interiors and tropical modernism,” George said.

The extension’s floor level steps upwards to meet the back of the main home, where the connecting corridor has been flanked by a bathroom and pantry space.

OEB Architects was founded in 2018 by directors Tessa Baird and David Eland, and has offices in London and Brighton. The studio previously completed another extension for a home in Tottenham, which expanded a semi-detached home with a simple blockwork and timber structure.
